The role demanded sustained attention to long coordination meetings, meticulous follow-through on complex task chains, and constant juggling of subcontractors, permitting, and change orders, which exposed longstanding but previously manageable difficulties in staying focused and on track. As projects grew more intricate, he began missing critical details during briefings, reacting impulsively in meetings, and overcompensating with late-night reworking of schedules that still failed to prevent costly oversights, eventually resulting in a near-miss safety incident and formal complaints from both superiors and subcontractors. When an executive review questioned his suitability for the leadership track he’d worked years to attain and he noticed similar patterns affecting his volunteer mentoring commitments and personal relationships, he agreed to see a psychiatrist after a colleague in his trade association described being treated for adult ADHD with similar work struggles.\n- Chief Complaint: In long meetings and what the engineers just decided, even though I was really trying to pay attention.\n- S003 (Listening_Difficulty): My wife and the guys at work get mad because they’ll be telling me something important and halfway through I’ve drifted off, looking right at them but not actually taking in the words, so I end up asking them to repeat what they just said.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow every morning I’m tearing the house or my truck apart for my keys, notebook, or iPad, even though I swear I just had them in my hand a minute ago, and I’ve actually had to drive back across town more in the hallway because sitting still for that whole hour feels unbearable.\n- S015 (Excessive_Talking): Once I get going, I’ll just keep talking—about the job, about some story from the site—until I question is going, I’ll jump in with an answer before my boss is even finished talking, and sometimes I’ve completely misunderstood what he was asking.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting in line at the permit office or for my turn to speak on a conference call, I get really antsy and irritated, checking the time over and over and feeling like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if things don’t move faster.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ll walk into other people’s conversations or start giving advice on their part of the project without being asked, and I catch myself cutting them off mid-sentence because I feel this urge to jump in right away.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always missed details and half-finished tasks have led to formal complaints at work, a near-miss safety incident, and my boss questioning if I’m fit to keep running these big projects, and it’s also It's just that my struggles with staying focused, keeping organized, and following through make it really hard to actually engage in them the way I'd like to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having an inability to sit still for periods of time, especially in situations where you're expected to be quiet or seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, sitting still is really tough for me, especially in long meetings where staying seated for a whole hour feels unbearable, like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I'm constantly popping up to get coffee or 'check on things' in the hallway just to move around. I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. Waiting my turn is just as bad; whether it's in line at the permit office or on a conference call, I get so antsy and irritated I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often this impulsivity, like interrupting or blurting out answers, happens in your daily life, and has it been a persistent pattern throughout your life or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: It's a daily occurrence at work where I constantly blurt out answers before my boss finishes speaking and cut colleagues off mid-sentence. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. Two years ago, when I transitioned to managing these massive, complex construction projects, there was suddenly zero room for error. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home."
  }
]

What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor, I'm Willie. I work as a construction manager, and lately, I've been having a really hard time keeping my head in the game. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor, I'm Willie. I work as a construction manager, and lately, I've been having a really hard time keeping my head in the game. In long meetings and on-site, my mind keeps drifting and I just can’t seem to stay focused on what people are saying or what I’m supposed to be tracking.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what's going through your mind when it drifts off during meetings or on-site, is it worries, unrelated thoughts, or something else?\n\nPatient: It’s not really anxious worrying, but more like my mind just completely zones out or goes blank. I can be looking directly at a schedule on the screen or at someone speaking, genuinely trying to pay attention, and then suddenly realize ten minutes have passed and I have absolutely no idea what was just said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or enjoyment of activities that you normally find pleasurable, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I haven't really lost interest in my family, hobbies, or my mentoring work at all. It's just that my struggles with staying focused, keeping organized, and following through make it really hard to actually engage in them the way I'd like to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having an inability to sit still for periods of time, especially in situations where you're expected to be quiet or seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, sitting still is really tough for me, especially in long meetings where staying seated for a whole hour feels unbearable, like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I'm constantly popping up to get coffee or 'check on things' in the hallway just to move around. I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. Waiting my turn is just as bad; whether it's in line at the permit office or on a conference call, I get so antsy and irritated I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often this impulsivity, like interrupting or blurting out answers, happens in your daily life, and has it been a persistent pattern throughout your life or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: It's a daily occurrence at work where I constantly blurt out answers before my boss finishes speaking and cut colleagues off mid-sentence. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. Two years ago, when I transitioned to managing these massive, complex construction projects, there was suddenly zero room for error. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class and never finishing my worksheets, but also about me constantly talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your hyperactivity symptoms, such as feeling fidgety or restless, have changed or remained the same from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Back in elementary school, my hyperactivity was very external, like talking over other kids and being unable to finish my worksheets without constant supervision. Now, it has shifted to a deep internal restlessness where I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin when waiting in lines. It also shows up as an unbearable urge to get out of my seat during long meetings just to walk around or grab coffee.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your symptoms of inattention, such as daydreaming and difficulty following through on tasks, were as prominent in childhood as your impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms, or did they become more noticeable later in life?"

The role demanded sustained attention to long coordination meetings, meticulous follow-through on complex task chains, and constant juggling of subcontractors, permitting, and change orders, which exposed longstanding but previously manageable difficulties in staying focused and on track. As projects grew more intricate, he began missing critical details during briefings, reacting impulsively in meetings, and overcompensating with late-night reworking of schedules that still failed to prevent costly oversights, eventually resulting in a near-miss safety incident and formal complaints from both superiors and subcontractors. When an executive review questioned his suitability for the leadership track he’d worked years to attain and he noticed similar patterns affecting his volunteer mentoring commitments and personal relationships, he agreed to see a psychiatrist after a colleague in his trade association described being treated for adult ADHD with similar work struggles.\n- Chief Complaint: In long meetings and on site, my mind keeps drifting and I can’t seem to stay focused on what people are saying or what I’m supposed to be tracking.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S002 what the engineers just decided, even though I was really trying to pay attention.\n- S003 (Listening_Difficulty): My wife and the guys at work get mad because they’ll be telling me something important and halfway through I’ve drifted off, looking right at them but not actually taking in the words, so I end up asking them to repeat what they just said.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start updating a project plan or writing up a change order, get about three-quarters done, then hop over to put out some other fire and never circle back, so there are all just kind of freeze because I can’t figure out what to tackle first, so I bounce between things and then end up scrambling at the last minute to meet deadlines.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): Pretty much every morning I’m tearing the house or my truck apart for my keys, notebook, or iPad, even though I swear I just had them in my hand a minute ago, and I’ve actually had to drive back across town more than once because I left crucial plans on a job site.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): In the longer meetings at the office I can’t stay in my chair, so I keep popping up to refill coffee or ‘check something’ in the hallway because sitting still for that whole hour feels unbearable.\n- S015 (Excessive_Talking): Once I get going, I’ll just keep talking—about the job, about some story from the site—until I notice people looking glazed over, and even then I have a hard time shutting up or letting someone else get a word in.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings, as soon as I think I know where a question is going, I’ll jump in with an answer before my boss is even finished talking, and sometimes I’ve completely misunderstood what he was asking.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting in line at the permit office or for my turn to speak on a conference call, I get really antsy and irritated, checking the time over and over and feeling like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if things don’t move faster.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ll walk into other people’s conversations or start giving advice on their part of the project without being asked, and I catch myself cutting them off mid-sentence because I feel this urge to jump in right away.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class, talking over other kids, and never finishing my worksheets unless someone stood over me.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past couple of years, my missed details and half-finished tasks have led to formal complaints at work, a near-miss safety incident, and my boss questioning if I’m fit to keep running these big projects, and it’s also straining things at home because my wife feels like she can’t rely on me to follow through.\n- duration: These problems have really ramped up and been a constant issue ever since I moved into managing Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor, I'm Willie. I work as a construction manager, and lately, I've been having a really hard time keeping my head in the game. In long meetings and on-site, my mind keeps drifting and I just can’t seem to stay focused on what people are saying or what I’m supposed to be tracking.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what's going through your mind when it drifts off during meetings or on-site, is it worries, unrelated thoughts, or something else?\n\nPatient: It’s not really anxious worrying, but more like my mind just completely zones out or goes blank. I can be looking directly at a schedule on the screen or at someone speaking, genuinely trying to pay attention, and then suddenly realize ten minutes have passed and I have absolutely no idea what was just said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or enjoyment of activities that you normally find pleasurable, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I haven't really lost interest in my family, hobbies, or my mentoring work at all. It's just that my struggles with staying focused, keeping organized, and following through make it really hard to actually engage in them the way I'd like to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having an inability to sit still for periods of time, especially in situations where you're expected to be quiet or seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, sitting still is really tough for me, especially in long meetings where staying seated for a whole hour feels unbearable, like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I'm constantly popping up to get coffee or 'check on things' in the hallway just to move around. I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. Waiting my turn is just as bad; whether it's in line at the permit office or on a conference call, I get so antsy and irritated I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often this impulsivity, like interrupting or blurting out answers, happens in your daily life, and has it been a persistent pattern throughout your life or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: It's a daily occurrence at work where I constantly blurt out answers before my boss finishes speaking and cut colleagues off mid-sentence. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. Two years ago, when I transitioned to managing these massive, complex construction projects, there was suddenly zero room for error. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class and never finishing my worksheets, but also about me constantly talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your hyperactivity symptoms, such as feeling fidgety or restless, have changed or remained the same from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Back in elementary school, my hyperactivity was very external, like talking over other kids and being unable to finish my worksheets without constant supervision. Now, it has shifted to a deep internal restlessness where I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin when waiting in lines. It also shows up as an unbearable urge to get out of my seat during long meetings just to walk around or grab coffee.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your symptoms of inattention, such as daydreaming and difficulty following through on tasks, were as prominent in childhood as your impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms, or did they become more noticeable later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were definitely just as prominent back in childhood. My elementary school teachers would constantly send notes home about both sides of the issue, complaining about my daydreaming and never finishing worksheets, as well as me talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any formal diagnosis or treatment for these symptoms, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and if so, what was the outcome?"
  }

My elementary school teachers would constantly send notes home about both sides of the issue, complaining about my daydreaming and never finishing worksheets, as well as me talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any formal diagnosis or treatment for these symptoms, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and if so, what was the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been formally diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, even though my teachers pointed out my daydreaming and talking back in grade school. I'm only seeking help now because a colleague in my trade association described being treated for adult ADHD, and his struggles sounded exactly like what I've been going through."
  }

What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor, I'm Willie. I work as a construction manager, and lately, I've been having a really hard time keeping my head in the game. In long meetings and on-site, my mind keeps drifting and I just can’t seem to stay focused on what people are saying or what I’m supposed to be tracking.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what's going through your mind when it drifts off during meetings or on-site, is it worries, unrelated thoughts, or something else?\n\nPatient: It’s not really anxious worrying, but more like my mind just completely zones out or goes blank. I can be looking directly at a schedule on the screen or at someone speaking, genuinely trying to pay attention, and then suddenly realize ten minutes have passed and I have absolutely no idea what was just said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or enjoyment of activities that you normally find pleasurable, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I haven't really lost interest in my family, hobbies, or my mentoring work at all. It's just that my struggles with staying focused, keeping organized, and following through make it really hard to actually engage in them the way I'd like to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having an inability to sit still for periods of time, especially in situations where you're expected to be quiet or seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, sitting still is really tough for me, especially in long meetings where staying seated for a whole hour feels unbearable, like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I'm constantly popping up to get coffee or 'check on things' in the hallway just to move around. I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. Waiting my turn is just as bad; whether it's in line at the permit office or on a conference call, I get so antsy and irritated I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often this impulsivity, like interrupting or blurting out answers, happens in your daily life, and has it been a persistent pattern throughout your life or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: It's a daily occurrence at work where I constantly blurt out answers before my boss finishes speaking and cut colleagues off mid-sentence. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. Two years ago, when I transitioned to managing these massive, complex construction projects, there was suddenly zero room for error. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class and never finishing my worksheets, but also about me constantly talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your hyperactivity symptoms, such as feeling fidgety or restless, have changed or remained the same from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Back in elementary school, my hyperactivity was very external, like talking over other kids and being unable to finish my worksheets without constant supervision. Now, it has shifted to a deep internal restlessness where I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin when waiting in lines. It also shows up as an unbearable urge to get out of my seat during long meetings just to walk around or grab coffee.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your symptoms of inattention, such as daydreaming and difficulty following through on tasks, were as prominent in childhood as your impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms, or did they become more noticeable later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were definitely just as prominent back in childhood. My elementary school teachers would constantly send notes home about both sides of the issue, complaining about my daydreaming and never finishing worksheets, as well as me talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any formal diagnosis or treatment for these symptoms, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and if so, what was the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been formally diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, even though my teachers pointed out my daydreaming and talking back in grade school. I'm only seeking help now because a colleague in my trade association described being treated for adult ADHD, and his struggles sounded exactly like what I've been going through.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant difficulties with organization, time management, or self-regulation of emotions, aside from the focus and impulsivity issues you've described, and if so, how have you coped with them?"

I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. Waiting my turn is just as bad; whether it's in line at the permit office or on a conference call, I get so antsy and irritated I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often this impulsivity, like interrupting or blurting out answers, happens in your daily life, and has it been a persistent pattern throughout your life or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: It's a daily occurrence at work where I constantly blurt out answers before my boss finishes speaking and cut colleagues off mid-sentence. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. Two years ago, when I transitioned to managing these massive, complex construction projects, there was suddenly zero room for error. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class and never finishing my worksheets, but also about me constantly talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your hyperactivity symptoms, such as feeling fidgety or restless, have changed or remained the same from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Back in elementary school, my hyperactivity was very external, like talking over other kids and being unable to finish my worksheets without constant supervision. Now, it has shifted to a deep internal restlessness where I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin when waiting in lines. It also shows up as an unbearable urge to get out of my seat during long meetings just to walk around or grab coffee.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your symptoms of inattention, such as daydreaming and difficulty following through on tasks, were as prominent in childhood as your impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms, or did they become more noticeable later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were definitely just as prominent back in childhood. My elementary school teachers would constantly send notes home about both sides of the issue, complaining about my daydreaming and never finishing worksheets, as well as me talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any formal diagnosis or treatment for these symptoms, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and if so, what was the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been formally diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, even though my teachers pointed out my daydreaming and talking back in grade school. I'm only seeking help now because a colleague in my trade association described being treated for adult ADHD, and his struggles sounded exactly like what I've been going through.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant difficulties with organization, time management, or self-regulation of emotions, aside from the focus and impulsivity issues you've described, and if so, how have you coped with them?\n\nPatient: Organization and time management are huge struggles; when I look at all the moving parts of these big projects, I just freeze because I don't know where to start, which leads to me bouncing between tasks and scrambling at the last minute. I've tried coping by staying up late to rework schedules, but it hasn't stopped the mistakes. Emotionally, I don't have major outbursts, but I get incredibly antsy and irritated whenever I'm forced to wait."
  }
]

When things start going wrong or deadlines are missed, I tend to freeze up because I honestly don't know what to tackle first. I can be looking directly at a schedule on the screen or at someone speaking, genuinely trying to pay attention, and then suddenly realize ten minutes have passed and I have absolutely no idea what was just said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or enjoyment of activities that you normally find pleasurable, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I haven't really lost interest in my family, hobbies, or my mentoring work at all. It's just that my struggles with staying focused, keeping organized, and following through make it really hard to actually engage in them the way I'd like to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having an inability to sit still for periods of time, especially in situations where you're expected to be quiet or seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, sitting still is really tough for me, especially in long meetings where staying seated for a whole hour feels unbearable, like I'm going to crawl out of my skin. I'm constantly popping up to get coffee or 'check on things' in the hallway just to move around. I also get incredibly antsy and keep checking the time whenever I have to wait in lines or for my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers before questions have been completed, or having difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do this constantly and it's incredibly frustrating. In meetings, I'll blurt out answers before my boss or coworkers even finish their questions, which means I often completely misunderstand them, and I'm always cutting people off mid-sentence or jumping into conversations. While this has become much more of a problem over the last two years with my new responsibilities, it's actually a lifelong pattern for me. Even back in elementary school, my teachers were constantly sending notes home because I would talk over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that interrupting others and blurting out answers has been a lifelong pattern, but you also said it's become more of a problem over the last two years. Can you tell me more about how your symptoms have changed or remained the same since childhood?\n\nPatient: Since childhood, I've always struggled with daydreaming, talking over other kids, and leaving tasks half-done, but I somehow managed to scrape by. That's when my lifelong habits of blurting things out, interrupting, and losing focus turned into a major crisis with serious consequences both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling excessively fidgety or restless, in childhood, or was it more prominent in attention difficulties and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both, even back in elementary school. Teachers were always sending notes home about me daydreaming in class and never finishing my worksheets, but also about me constantly talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your hyperactivity symptoms, such as feeling fidgety or restless, have changed or remained the same from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Back in elementary school, my hyperactivity was very external, like talking over other kids and being unable to finish my worksheets without constant supervision. Now, it has shifted to a deep internal restlessness where I feel like I'm going to crawl out of my skin when waiting in lines. It also shows up as an unbearable urge to get out of my seat during long meetings just to walk around or grab coffee.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your symptoms of inattention, such as daydreaming and difficulty following through on tasks, were as prominent in childhood as your impulsivity and hyperactivity symptoms, or did they become more noticeable later in life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they were definitely just as prominent back in childhood. My elementary school teachers would constantly send notes home about both sides of the issue, complaining about my daydreaming and never finishing worksheets, as well as me talking over the other kids.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any formal diagnosis or treatment for these symptoms, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, and if so, what was the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been formally diagnosed or treated for ADHD before, even though my teachers pointed out my daydreaming and talking back in grade school. I'm only seeking help now because a colleague in my trade association described being treated for adult ADHD, and his struggles sounded exactly like what I've been going through.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant difficulties with organization, time management, or self-regulation of emotions, aside from the focus and impulsivity issues you've described, and if so, how have you coped with them?\n\nPatient: Organization and time management are huge struggles; when I look at all the moving parts of these big projects, I just freeze because I don't know where to start, which leads to me bouncing between tasks and scrambling at the last minute. I've tried coping by staying up late to rework schedules, but it hasn't stopped the mistakes. Emotionally, I don't have major outbursts, but I get incredibly antsy and irritated whenever I'm forced to wait.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you handle stress or frustration when things don't go as planned, such as missing a deadline or making a mistake on a project?\n\nPatient: When things start going wrong or deadlines are missed, I tend to freeze up because I honestly don't know what to tackle first. I end up scrambling at the last minute and trying to overcompensate by staying up late at night to rework our schedules. Even with that extra effort, it usually still leads to costly oversights and mistakes on the job.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any instances where your hyperactivity symptoms, such as fidgeting or restlessness, have caused problems in your personal or professional life, and how you've tried to manage them?\n\nPatient: In long meetings, sitting still is so unbearable that I constantly get up to grab coffee or check the hallway just to cope with the restlessness, which looks really unprofessional. Also, waiting in lines at the permit office or waiting my turn on conference calls makes me so antsy and irritated that I feel like I'm crawling out of my skin, which has really strained my patience and professional relationships."

Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).
